## assistant

v3 is on the canvas, revised after astra's critique: https://claude.ai/artifact/6zF3FgewiMA7pCZSk766Xt. It's in the third row, titled "v3". As before, I haven't opened the canvas to check how it renders.

**How v3 differs from v2**
- **One large warm top panel** carries the page, like Homies Lab:
  - **Left:** the date, a large light serif greeting, and two big numbers (questions this week, days to test day).
  - **Right:** the search bar, a dark "Continue" card for the assignment due today, and small rounded buttons for Smart Drill, Timed section and Inbox · 3.
- **One large visual as the centerpiece**, like Be.run:
  - A taupe card, two thirds wide, headed "Assumption questions are costing you the most points."
  - Overlapping circles sized by questions missed, labeled by type and accuracy.
  - Buttons: "Practice Necessary Assumption" and "Review these".
  - Next to it, a dark Next session card with a big "7:15 pm" and bars for sessions left.
- **Mixed sizes below:**
  - A wide list of every question type with a precise bar, question count and change vs the previous 30 days, each with a Drill button.
  - Next to it, smaller tiles: last section (timed and blind review bars), your latest message, and wrong answers to revisit.
- **Next seven days:** unchanged.

**Two questions before I write the build spec:**

**Q1 - The left menu**: v3 draws a slim icon-only menu, like Be.run. The real menu is shared by every Classroom page, so changing it changes the whole Classroom, not just Home. Keep today's full menu, or slim it everywhere?

Recommended: keep today's menu for this build. Slimming it is its own project, with its own testing across every page.

---

**Q2 - Students with little data**: my proposed rule:
- With only one or two question types answered, show a single result with its question count instead of circles.
- With no practice at all, show a short invitation to start Smart Drill.
- With no session or no assignment, hide that card instead of showing an empty box.

Recommended: yes, those three rules.

Once you answer, I'll write the build spec, which covers this layout plus the insight numbers (question types and last section). gpt-6-sol at low effort will build it, and I'll review. PR #133 stays unmerged and gets replaced.

A metacognitive confidence calibration (MCC) tool to help ...
  URL: https://journals.physiology.org/doi/full/10.1152/advan.00156.2021
  ## Abstract
This result revealed overconfidence bias in novice students regarding the correctness of their wrong responses. This study demonstrated the feasibility of using a metacognitive confidence calibration tool to assist novice students in learning safely to make deliberate diagnostic reasoning and decisions on patient care in real time during complex simulations while observing objectively their levels of psychological confidence against patient outcomes.

## INTRODUCTION
To develop a strong metacognitive, critical reasoning capacity, medical students need to both _1_) revise their confidence level in every decision, or learning response, in the context of the relevant piece of training in a progression of expertise development and _2_) be able to learn productively from mistakes in different case scenarios, in practice, while not harming patients.

## MATERIALS AND METHODS
### Rationale for Development of the MCC Tool
Although overconfidence bias may not be easily eliminated given its unconscious entrenchment, support for the deliberate practice of diagnostic reasoning and for the deliberate calibration of one’s overconfidence in diagnostic reasoning against patient outcomes may provide learners with better means and enough feedback for the development of appropriate self-efficacy and reflective expertise.

## RESULTS
### Student Accuracy Findings
Those students also displayed inappropriate metacognitive tracking of their reasoning and level of performance, Such _z_ scores provide a novel way to illuminate student performance and support student learning with personalized feedback on zones of confidence-accuracy for improving metacognitive understanding of one’s hot cognition and its performative outcomes in the diagnosis learning context of HFPS.

## REFERENCES
The metacognitive advantage of deliberative thinkers: a dual-process perspective on overconfidence.

# How Does WHOOP Recovery Work?

By WHOOP

March 30, 2022

[Share on Facebook](https://www.facebook.com/sharer/sharer.php?u=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.whoop.com%2Fus%2Fen%2Fthelocker%2Fhow-does-whoop-recovery-work-101%2F "Share on Facebook")[Share on Twitter](https://twitter.com/intent/tweet?text=How%20Does%20WHOOP%20Recovery%20Work%3F&url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.whoop.com%2Fus%2Fen%2Fthelocker%2Fhow-does-whoop-recovery-work-101%2F "Share on Twitter")

![](https://images.ctfassets.net/rbzqg6pelgqa/ruIw9Z1x8067nr7ovnTvE/67e018f8ffd6f47c36cc99a16771746f/How-does-WHOOP-recovery-work-HEADER-1800x1000__1_.jpg?fm=webp&q=25&w=3840)

Recovery 101: Everything you need to know about how WHOOP measures your body’s readiness to perform.

Below we’ll dive into Recovery, what it means, how it’s calculated, what you can do to improve it, the relationship between Recovery and Strain, plus how to figure out which choices and behaviors impact your recovery the most.

## What is WHOOP Recovery?

WHOOP recovery is a daily measure of how prepared your body is to perform, no matter what it is that you do. It quantifies how your body is adapting to various stressors–for everything from training for elite athletic competition, to even just working from home in your sweats. After you get up each morning WHOOP calculates your recovery as a percentage, and categorizes it as green, yellow, or re

![](https://images.ctfassets.net/rbzqg6pelgqa/2Ily7LHAyCbEVqOor7BUDt/3318a5359e15a592b8da1dce1dc9b4cf/Recovery-Dials-1-768x363.png?fm=webp&q=25&w=1920)

## WHOOP Recovery is a percentage color-coded as green, yellow or red.

**GREEN (67-99%):** You are well recovered and primed to perform. Whether it’s at home, at work, or in the gym, your body is signaling that it can handle a strenuous day. **YELLOW (34-66%):** Your body is maintaining and ready to take on moderate amounts of strain. **RED (1-33%):** Rest is likely what your body needs. It is working hard to recover, potentially from overtraining, sickness, stress

## How Does WHOOP Calculate Recovery?

WHOOP incorporates several key physiological metrics into your daily recovery: **Heart Rate Variability (HRV):** Literally the variance in time between your heart beats, HRV is a benchmark of your autonomic nervous system that is very useful for evaluating physical fitness. To learn more, check out our [_Ultimate Guide to HRV_](https://www.whoop.com/us/en/thelocker/heart-rate-variability-hrv/). **

![](https://images.ctfassets.net/rbzqg6pelgqa/3wYhQu8tdevI6cE1FE82VT/a1f2e3b895729dcbc43d2fb468b346b4/How-does-WHOOP-recovery-wor-INLINE-1000x1000-1__1_.jpg?fm=webp&q=25&w=2048)

HRV, Resting heart rate, Sleep & Respiratory Rate are all Factored into your daily recovery. [Blood oxygen level](https://www.whoop.com/us/en/thelocker/measure-normal-blood-oxygen-levels/) and [skin temperature](https://www.whoop.com/us/en/thelocker/what-is-skin-temperature-and-why-should-you-monitor-it/) are included in your recovery as well and can help provide a better understanding of when you

## Recovery & Strain

How recovered you are each morning determines your capability to take on [strain](https://www.whoop.com/us/en/thelocker/how-does-whoop-strain-work-101/) that day–the better your recovery, the more strain your body is prepared for.

![](https://images.ctfassets.net/rbzqg6pelgqa/1746IxeV9WZEamrPM3IdFm/12e21fecba58229ffb1fe5de6362a836/How-does-WHOOP-recovery-wor-INLINE-1000x1000-2.jpg?fm=webp&q=25&w=2048)

Your Recovery dictates how much strain your body is ready to handle. Based on your recovery, WHOOP recommends an optimal strain each day. It’s a suggested amount intended to allow you to maintain fitness while still enabling your body to adequately recover (go above and you are “overreaching,” stay below and you are “restoring”).

![](https://images.ctfassets.net/rbzqg6pelgqa/4uldPbRT558oVp2k9aHnGP/a2ff33a43cdf76924e70e71965b3a42c/How-does-WHOOP-recovery-wor-INLINE-1000x1000-3.jpg?fm=webp&q=25&w=2048)

#### [**WEEKLY PERFORMANCE ASSESSMENTS**](https://www.whoop.com/us/en/thelocker/new-weekly-performance-assessment/) **display the balance between your Recovery & Strain.**

[_Learn More: Training Zones_](https://www.whoop.com/us/en/thelocker/whoop-training-zones-optimal-overreaching-restoring/)

## Training Based on Your Recovery

A 2020 study found that [runners who adjusted their workouts](https://www.whoop.com/us/en/thelocker/project-pr-runner-study/) depending on their WHOOP recoveries (increased activity duration and intensity when in the green, reduced when in the red) experienced fewer injuries and spent less time training than those who did not–all while making equal fitness gains. Using WHOOP recovery as a guide 

## How Can I Improve My Recovery?

Beyond **getting all the sleep you need** at night and generally engaging in healthy behaviors, here are several habits that can help boost you recovery:

# Metacognitive Monitoring: Fixing Learner Overconfidence

[**Paul Main**](https://www.structural-learning.com/team/paulmain)

**\|**

March 4, 2026

Why learners overestimate what they know, and how to fix it. Research-backed calibration strategies from Hacker (1998) and the Dunning-Kruger effect.

[Start your metacognitive learning plan](https://app.structural-learning.com/auth/register?plan=free&utm_source=blog&utm_medium=blog_link&utm_campaign=general_pedagogy&utm_content=metacognitive-monitoring-fixing-student&utm_term=blog_cta&cta_slot=blog_link&cta_variant=blog_cta&from=blog_link&source_page=%2Fpost%2Fmetacognitive-monitoring-fixing-student)

[Copy citation](https://www.structural-learning.com/post/metacognitive-monitoring-fixing-student#)

Main, P. (2026, March 4). Metacognitive Monitoring: Fixing Learner Overconfidence. Structural Learning. https://www.structural-learning.com/post/metacognitive-monitoring-fixing-student

Metacognitive monitoring is a learner's running estimate of how well they understand something. Overconfidence is fixed by calibration: before a quiz, learners write a predicted score, you mark it, they write the actual score beside it, and the gap is tracked until confidence follows evidence.

**Metacognitive monitoring** is the judgement a learner makes about what they know while they learn. The fix for overconfidence is to check that judgement against a real result. Most learners rate themselves too high, and the weakest do so most (Kruger & Dunning, 1999).

So ask for a score before a task, mark it, and put the two side by side. Repeat that until confidence tracks evidence. [Classroom calibration training](https://www.structural-learning.com/post/illusion-of-knowing-calibration) and feedback reduce overconfidence in every subject, and the routines below fit inside ordinary lessons.

# LSAT Tips: Perfecting Blind Review

BY

Raffi Piliero


Published Apr 13, 2022


# Quick Tip

## Skipping Questions

Many students are unsure about when to skip a question, sometimes feeling as though time up front on a question means they ought to try to finish it. To some extent, this reflects “sunk cost fallacy,” the tendency to want to spend additional time on something because time was spent at the outset.

Instead, think of time on a question as characterized by diminishing marginal returns—every additional second you spend on a “hard question” is increasingly likely to prove ineffective if the question is particularly challenging. Instead, you should work on setting a “stop-loss” for when to move on. After spending a particular amount of time (say, one minute) staring at a question, force

# Discussion

## Optimizing Blind Review

Blind Review (BR) is 7Sage’s way of separating fundamentals from technique, or untimed from timed performance. In other words, you use the BR process to see if your problem is knowing the material or simply not knowing it fast enough.

This is valuable because the insights gleaned from it can help you allocate your study time. If your BR score is significantly higher than your untimed score, that suggests you ought to do more timed sections and drills to build speed. If, on the other hand, your BR score isn’t that much higher than your untimed score, your fundamentals are what need work; this might counsel in favor of reviewin

Many students understand the value of BR but fail to utilize it optimally, however. Here’s how I would BR, if I were studying today.

1. Take the section/test under timed conditions. No “cheating” with extra time or breaks or anything like that. Flag every question that you’re at all unsure about.
2. Take a break! Give your mind a chance to rest before BR. This also gives you a fresh perspective on the questions you’ve just done.
3. Start with questions you guessed on and didn’t answer, but then flip through the entire section. For unflagged questions, quickly confirm your answer and check that you didn’t misread something. For flagged questions, go through them meticulously. Redo the questions and work through them until you can start to make sense of them, taking the time that you need; however, try to limit yourself
4. Score the section and add missed (or flagged) questions into a Wrong Answer Journal.

The biggest issue I’ve noticed with students is rushing through BR. Generally speaking, this should take you about as long as it took you to do the test, but will vary depending on your score, which dictates how many questions you’d be unsure about. At the extremes, though, I would be surprised if a proper BR is ever less than one hour or longer than six hours (twice as long as a four-section 

In sum, BR can be an important part of any improvement plan—the alternative is taking a large quantity of material but not improving much due to a lack of introspection. Optimizing your BR routine can help you squeeze as much as possible out of any given test.

**My top 5, saved before seeing Codex's list:**
1. **Today's session.** Instead of a menu of options, the page gives each student a short plan for the day: "Today: about 35 minutes", with reviews due, the assignment, and a few Smart Drill questions on their weakest type, all under one Start button. This follows Whoop, which recommends a day's workload based on your recovery instead of offering a menu.
2. **Speed or understanding?** A verdict under the last-section tile. If blind review scores well above the timed score, the student knows the material and needs speed, so practice timed. If there's little gap, the problem is understanding, so review first. It's 7Sage's own rule, but no dashboard works it out for the student.
3. **Sure but wrong.** A small chart of confidence against correctness: "You were sure on 9 questions you missed", with a Review button. The app already records confidence on every question and has never shown it.
4. **The coach on the page.**
   - Your pinned note sits on the student's insight card, in your words.
   - An "Ask" button on any insight sends you the evidence behind it.
   - The day before a session, a "Bring to Thursday" list collects their flagged and sure-but-wrong questions, and you see it too.
   - Self-serve prep companies can't copy this.
5. **Craft you can feel.**
   - Home appears instantly from the last visit instead of loading.
   - Placeholder shapes match the real layout instead of spinners.
   - Numbers count up only on the first visit of the day.
   - Opening a card animates into the drill.
   - Keyboard shortcuts.

**Blind spots I found:**
- The page doesn't change as test day approaches: build, then timed sections and review, then taper.
- Meaning shown by color alone, which fails for color-blind students.
- Numbers with no "what it counts" or "last updated".
- No real phone layout.
- You can't see exactly what a student sees.

# Five ways to make v3 a better learning dashboard

2026-09-23 · Independent research; no other researcher consulted.

**My view:** preserve v3’s visual character and make every prominent element connect evidence to a useful action. The reference images support hierarchy, atmosphere, and varied scale; they do not establish learning effectiveness. All five recommendations sharpen existing cards. Keep the warm palette, dark anchors, one inbox, fixed-rule insights, coach pinning, and the seven-day strip unchanged.

Evidence below supports principles or product precedents, not proven LSAT U outcomes. Paths are relative to `C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/projects/lsatu/`. Sizes, thresholds, and motion timings are proposed specifications. Effort includes missing integration work.

## 1. Prescribe the practice, not just the question type

**Student sees:** Within the existing 420px main insight, reserve a 64–88px action block: “Rebuild the assumption test” → “Open the lesson.” Show a short, fixed explanation tied to the saved finding. A fundamentals finding routes to a mapped bootcamp lesson; a process finding to a guided drill; a reading finding to Read, Recall, Colour; a test-taking finding to a section briefing. Keep the existing alternative practice/review links and top actions. The action block crossfades in 150ms; no automatic movement while reading. Without a supported mapping, show “Review the diagnosis.” Coach-pinned state names Joshua explicitly.

**Why world-class:** The dashboard makes an instructional decision, rather than leaving the student to translate a chart. [Ericsson et al.](https://graphics8.nytimes.com/images/blogs/freakonomics/pdf/DeliberatePractice(PsychologicalReview).pdf) describe deliberate practice as structured work aimed at particular improvements. This extends the plan’s diagnosis-to-filter link to different learning activities.

**Existing foundation:** `src/lib/sectionAnalysis.ts` already contains categories, prescriptions, process families, and supporting questions; `src/lib/sectionHistorySave.ts` saves `analysis_json`. Launches: `src/lib/drillLaunch.ts`; recall activities: `src/features/activity-centre/api/types.ts`. **New:** a coach-reviewed diagnosis-to-resource registry; never infer a lesson from free text.

**Effort: M. Risk:** an inaccurate diagnosis can prescribe the wrong work. Expose the finding, preserve alternatives, and let Joshua’s pin win.

## 2. Turn “revisit” into a learning loop with an ending

**Student sees:** Expand v3’s amber wrong-answer strip to approximately 112px: “Revisit 3 questions” with three quiet stages: **Recall → Reattempt → Try a new question**. Launch the existing player; explanations stay concealed until the student attempts the question. After the due reviews, offer one fresh, matching question as a separate step. Return Home to “3 revisited · fresh check: 1/1,” never “mastered.” In-progress work resumes; no due items yields “Nothing scheduled for review.” A saved completion produces one 180ms check transition, no celebration.

**Why world-class:** It adds retrieval and transfer checking to the already-planned due queue. [Roediger and Karpicke](https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1111/j.1467-9280.2006.01693.x) found better delayed recall after testing than restudying prose. [Khan Academy](https://support.khanacademy.org/hc/en-us/articles/360037127892-What-are-Mastery-Challenges-in-course-mastery) revisits previously practised skills according to elapsed time and mastery. The fresh-question check is my proposed LSAT adaptation, not a validated mastery test.

**Existing foundation:** `src/lib/wajNotesService.ts` supplies due dates, next actions, and lessons; `src/hooks/useJournalEntries.ts` supplies entries; `src/lib/drillLaunch.ts` supports question lists and filters. **New:** durable loop/outcome records and exposure-aware fresh selection. Journal status alone cannot prove delayed retention.

**Effort: L. Risk:** remembered answer letters masquerading as understanding; keep repeat and fresh results separate and never auto-promote journal mastery.

## 3. Show the gap between confidence and correctness

**Student sees:** An eligible 220px-high minor insight beneath the main card, using four labelled cells: **Sure/right, Sure/wrong, Unsure/right, Unsure/wrong**. Example: “You rated 3 missed questions 4–5/5. Revisit those decisions.” Tap a cell to open its evidence list, then the question. Counts appear immediately; selected-cell emphasis fades in 120ms. Use 1–2 as unsure, 4–5 as sure; show neutral/missing counts in a coverage line. Hide the lens below a provisional 12 distinct rated initial attempts.

**Why world-class:** Accuracy misses the distinction between uncertainty and a confident misconception. [Metcalfe and Finn](https://www.columbia.edu/cu/psychology/metcalfe/PDFs/MetcalfeFinn2011.pdf) studied correction of high-confidence errors after feedback. Their general-knowledge findings motivate investigation; they do not establish an LSAT benefit.

**Existing foundation:** `src/pages/Drill.tsx` writes correctness, confidence, mode, and time to `attempts`; `src/components/drill/ConfidenceSelector.tsx` uses 1–5. **New:** an aggregation adapter restricted to verified pre-feedback ratings; some write paths currently save null.

**Effort: M. Risk:** sparse or post-feedback ratings. Exclude unknown timing, disclose coverage, and avoid personality labels such as “overconfident.”

## 4. Make the next session a prepared conversation

**Student sees:** Inside the existing dark session card, add an 88px “Bring to Joshua” preview above its buttons: up to three student-selected question addresses and one short note. “Prepare session” opens a drawer with flagged questions, relevant evidence, and a link to the last published recap. States: empty invitation, private draft, explicitly shared, session rescheduled. Saving uses a quiet inline confirmation; the drawer opens in 180ms. Any message goes through the existing inbox with an explicit Send action.

**Why world-class:** It connects independent work to the service students actually buy. [Things](https://culturedcode.com/things/support/articles/4001304/) brings calendar context and actionable tasks together; [Linear’s inbox](https://linear.app/docs/inbox) lets people inspect the underlying work directly. The coaching benefit is a design hypothesis worth testing with Joshua and students.

**Existing foundation:** `src/lib/flaggedService.ts` supplies flags and notes; `src/pages/ClassroomRecaps.tsx` reads published recaps; `src/components/classroom/home/homeModel.ts` supplies session context; `src/hooks/useInbox.ts` supports messaging. **New:** agenda selection/draft storage keyed to booking, plus coach visibility.

**Effort: M. Risk:** another coach queue. Keep one brief per booking, accessible from session details; never treat every flag as an agenda item.

## 5. Make the beautiful bubbles explain themselves

**Student sees:** Preserve v3’s main bubble composition. Add a 32px “Missed questions” legend with two reference sizes. Bubble **area**, not diameter, encodes misses; every bubble names its denominator. Tap or keyboard-select one to highlight its existing type row below and update the action block. Use the existing rows for exact comparisons; on narrow containers, expose those values without overlapping labels. No eligible evidence means no bubbles. Selection gets a 150ms outline fade; resizing never animates statistical change.

**Why world-class:** [Datawrapper](https://www.datawrapper.de/blog/chart-types-guide) distinguishes expressive proportional-area graphics from easier-to-compare bars. V3 can have both. Change “costing you the most points” to “Most misses in your recent LR practice”: adaptive sampling does not establish lost exam points. Suppress trend arrows when modes or difficulty mix are materially different; name the mismatch.

**Existing foundation:** `src/pages/Drill.tsx` records question type, difficulty, mode, and correctness. The plan’s distinct-question RPC is **planned, not existing**. `src/lib/sectionAnalysis.ts` already models evidence-bearing breakdowns.

**Effort: M. Risk:** precision theatre. Counts describe sampled work; neither large bubbles nor tiny percentage changes establish ability.

## Blind spots

1. **Adaptive instability:** freeze insight order during a visit; offer an explicit refresh after new work. Background updates must not move a focused action. This adds to the plan’s fixed selection rules.
2. **Accommodation-aware interpretation:** pace findings must use the student’s actual timer allowance. Extend the plan’s timer preservation into every comparison, not just launches.
3. **Beyond keyboard testing:** check screen-reader chart alternatives, 200% text enlargement, contrast on orange gradients, and focus restoration from drawers. [WCAG 2.2](https://www.w3.org/TR/WCAG22/) covers these concerns; reduced motion alone is insufficient.
4. **Evidence lifecycle:** distinguish loading, failed, stale, and truly empty sources. Preserve the last successful result with its date; an RPC failure must not tell a returning student to “Start here.”
5. **Success criteria:** test time to appropriate practice, delayed-review completion, fresh-question performance, and coaching preparation. Dashboard engagement alone cannot validate learning.

## Considered and rejected

- **Readiness score:** [Strava’s fitness/freshness](https://stories.strava.com/articles/how-to-use-stravas-fitness-and-freshness-tool) is an appealing metaphor. LSAT U lacks a validated equivalent; a composite number would overclaim.
- **Persistent animated glows:** keep atmospheric styling, cut continuous attention-seeking motion. [NN/g](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/animation-usability/) explains its distraction cost.
- **Another command surface:** [Raycast Quicklinks](https://www.raycast.com/core-features/quicklinks) are useful precedent, but the planned Question Finder already owns this job.
- **An unrestricted widget builder:** [Notion’s task aggregation](https://www.notion.com/help/guides/give-your-to-dos-a-home-with-task-databases) is useful; requiring students to design Home adds configuration work and weakens coaching priorities.

Research used Firecrawl searches and primary-page scrapes; paid product flows were not tested. Stripe’s scrape was CAPTCHA-blocked and supplies no relied-on evidence. Source captures remain in `.firecrawl/`.

Claude’s strongest contribution is making coaching visible inside the work. Its weakest is turning blind-review score differences into a confident diagnosis.

**Grades for Claude’s five ideas**

1. **Today’s session — B:** A finishable sequence could reduce indecision, but WHOOP is an analogy rather than learning evidence, and prescribing “Smart Drill on Necessary Assumption” conflicts with LSAT U’s distinction between adaptive Smart Drill and filtered practice.
2. **Speed or understanding? — D:** Relevant question, unjustified verdict: a score gap alone cannot establish the cause of mistakes, the thresholds lack support, and LSAT U already has a richer question-level diagnosis engine.
3. **Sure but wrong — B+:** Useful, well matched to existing data, and directionally supported by calibration research, but “overconfidence is the most common study failure” is unsupported and the proposal leaves rating definitions and collection timing unresolved.
4. **The coach is on the page — A−:** Signed pin notes and evidence-attached questions fit paid tutoring exceptionally well, but automatic agenda collection needs student control and the claimed competitive exclusivity is unsubstantiated.
5. **Craft you can feel — B−:** Fast, stable loading and keyboard access matter, but the evidence does not establish that count-ups or shared-element transitions improve studying, and “instant” promises more than caching guarantees.

**Claude’s blind spots**

- **Test-day phases — C:** Calendar context is real; automatic “14 days = timed sections” and “3 days = no new material” are unsupported prescriptions that could override the student’s actual needs. Make this coach-controlled.
- **Color-only meaning — B:** Valid audit requirement, overstated as a discovery: v3 already includes numeric changes, type labels, and counts. Check every state rather than assume the entire design relies on colour.
- **Freshness and trust — A−:** Update timestamps and stale-state handling are real omissions; denominators and coverage labels are already explicit in the plan.
- **Phone first — B+:** A deliberate phone composition matters, but responsive/container-width work is already planned. The useful addition is prioritising what appears before the first scroll.
- **Joshua’s mirror — A:** A strong addition: Joshua needs the student’s actual selected insight, evidence, and pin state, not merely a similarly styled teacher dashboard.

**What I would steal—and reject**

I would steal **the signed note on a pinned insight, plus Ask with attached evidence**. It gives Joshua’s judgment a precise location and lets students ask about the actual finding without reconstructing it in a message.

I reject **the blind-review verdict as written**. “You know more than the clock lets you show” overstates what the score difference proves. Additional time, reconsideration, and question-specific errors need examination. Three sections reduce noise; they do not validate the causal inference.

**Where we overlap**

- **Practice prescription:** Mine is better grounded: route a saved diagnosis to the appropriate learning activity. Claude’s sequence is better at defining a stopping point. Combine those strengths without forcing assignments into step two.
- **Confidence calibration:** Mine is stronger on evidence and data integrity: explicit buckets, missing ratings, initial attempts, and cautious interpretation. Claude’s headline is shorter and more immediately actionable.
- **Coaching:** Claude wins on bringing Joshua into the insight itself; mine wins on deliberate agenda selection, draft/share states, and workload boundaries.
- **Craft and trust:** Claude gives performance deserved prominence. Mine better addresses stale evidence, misleading charts, and layout stability. Neither list demonstrates student outcomes yet.

**Merged TOP 7, ranked**

1. **Diagnosis-to-action:** Turn the main insight into a justified next step: lesson, guided practice, recall activity, review, or timed section.
2. **Coaching attached to evidence:** Add Joshua’s signed pin note and an Ask action that drafts a message with the relevant evidence.
3. **Review that checks learning:** Connect due reattempts to a separate fresh-question check, preserving the distinction between remembering and transferring.
4. **A finishable practice sequence:** Offer a resumable, optional sequence with a clear endpoint; preserve assignment priority and honest duration estimates.
5. **Confidence calibration:** Surface confidently missed and uncertainly correct questions using verified pre-feedback ratings.
6. **Truthful visual evidence:** Keep the bubbles, but correct their encoding, expose denominators, and qualify comparisons.
7. **Fast, stable, accessible Home:** Design loading, stale, error, mobile, keyboard, and reduced-motion states as carefully as the populated desktop.

I accept moving the finishable sequence to third. I still put diagnosis-to-action first.

**Coaching is the strongest differentiator; diagnosis is the strongest daily function.** A student opening Home between sessions needs a defensible next action. A signed note, contextual question, or prepared agenda strengthens that decision, but does not necessarily supply it. Coaching deserves first place if student research shows that uncertainty about Joshua’s direction is the dominant problem. We have not established that. “No competitor can offer it” is also unnecessary to the argument; contextual coaching can be valuable without being exclusive.

**The sequence is useful, but it is not a prerequisite.** The existing main insight can host the diagnosis action; the existing review strip can host the review loop. Neither requires another card. Joshua rejected visual uniformity, which is not the same as rejecting separate functions.

I move the sequence up because it can answer both “Where do I start?” and “When am I finished?” Its value depends on restraint: optional, resumable, no forced review-before-assignment order, and no automatic reshuffling on focus. Start with one recommended task and disclose the remaining sequence beneath it. A three-step daily prescription should earn its complexity through student use.

**Final ranked seven I can sign off on**

1. **Diagnosis-to-action:** Make the main insight recommend the right learning activity, supported by question-level evidence and a small, Joshua-reviewed resource registry.
2. **Coaching attached to the work:** Combine signed pin notes, Ask with evidence, and one student-selected session brief with explicit draft/shared states.
3. **A finishable practice sequence:** Offer an optional, persistent path through appropriate work, with a clear endpoint and preserved direct-launch actions.
4. **Review followed by a fresh check:** Separate successful reattempts from performance on unfamiliar questions; never equate either with mastery.
5. **Confidence calibration:** Identify useful review candidates from verified pre-feedback ratings, with explicit coverage and missing-data handling.
6. **Truthful visual evidence:** Preserve v3’s expressive charts while correcting scale, denominators, comparisons, and claims.
7. **Fast, stable, accessible Home:** Treat this as a release requirement across all six ideas, not work deferred until seventh.

**Unresolved: first place**

- **Claude:** Coaching first because it delivers the paid tutoring relationship.
- **Codex:** Diagnosis first because it guides independent practice; coaching strengthens and can override it.

**Unresolved: the sequence’s role**

- **Claude:** Today is the organising host for diagnosis and review.
- **Codex:** Today is an optional route through those functions; their existing placements must remain independently useful.

## assistant

The research is done. Both of us researched separately with Firecrawl, graded each other's lists, and argued to one ranked list. Everything is filed in `research/2026-09-23-classroom-home-dashboard-research/`, and the brainstorm file is updated.

**The agreed 7.** All of them add to v3; none replace it.
1. **Diagnosis to action.** The main insight doesn't just say "Necessary Assumption is weak". It recommends the right kind of work:
   - a bootcamp lesson if the section analysis says fundamentals
   - a guided drill if it says process
   - Read, Recall, Colour if it says reading
   - timed practice if it says test-taking

   The app already produces these four categories for every section.
2. **Coaching attached to the work.** Your signed note on a pinned insight, an "Ask" button that sends you the evidence behind it, and a short pre-session brief the student prepares for you.
3. **A "Today" sequence.** One optional, resumable path through the day's work, with a clear finish line.
4. **Review, then a fresh check.** Redo the missed question, then try an unseen question of the same kind. That separates "remembered the answer" from "learned the skill".
5. **Sure but wrong.** Questions they were confident about and missed, using the confidence ratings the app already saves.
6. **Honest visuals.** Keep the bubbles, but size them correctly, show the counts, and use plainer wording: "Most misses in your recent LR practice" instead of "costing you points".
7. **Fast, stable and accessible.** Instant loading, clear stale and error states, a real phone layout, screen readers, and your accommodation timers respected. This is a bar every feature must meet, not a separate feature.

**Where Codex beat me:**
- I withdrew my blind-review verdict card. A score gap alone doesn't prove whether the problem is speed or understanding, so the gap becomes evidence inside #1 instead.
- Codex also caught that "Smart Drill on one question type" isn't possible. It has to be a filtered drill.

**Where Codex missed something I caught:**
- Your view of exactly what a student sees.
- A "last updated" on every number.
- Codex took my signed-note idea.

**Round 10**

**Q1 - What comes first**: Codex and I disagree. It says diagnosis to action, because students need a clear next step between sessions. I say coaching attached to the work, because the 1:1 relationship is what students pay for. Which leads the page?

Recommended: coaching. Your note sits on top of the diagnosis, so both show, but your voice leads.

---

**Q2 - The role of "Today"**: Should it be the main path that holds the other features (my view), or an optional shortcut while each feature keeps its own spot (Codex's view)?

Recommended: optional shortcut for the first build. Codex is right that it has to prove itself, and the page stays useful without it.

---

**Q3 - Your part in diagnosis to action**: it needs a short list you approve, matching each of the four problem types to the lesson or drill you'd assign. Will you write it, starting with about 4 to 8 entries?

Recommended: yes. I'd draft it from your bootcamps and guided drills, and you'd correct it.

---

**Q4 - Confidence ratings**: "Sure but wrong" only works if students rate their confidence before they see the answer. Some parts of the app save no rating today. Should the drill always ask for one?

Recommended: ask on every drill question, as one optional tap that doesn't block the answer.

---

**Q5 - The session brief**: Before a session, a student picks up to 3 questions and adds a short note. It stays private until they tap Share, and then you see it in the Hub. Do you want this?

Recommended: yes.

---

**Q6 - Two questions from earlier:**
- (a) Keep today's full left menu instead of slimming it, since slimming changes every page.
- (b) With little data, show one result instead of the circles. With no practice, show an invitation to start. With no session or assignment, hide that card instead of showing an empty box.

Recommended: yes to both.

---

**Q7 - What goes in the first build**:

Recommended:
- **First build:** v3 layout, #6 honest visuals, #7 quality bar, #2 your note and Ask, #1 diagnosis to action, and #5 sure but wrong.
- **Second build:** the Today sequence, the session brief, and review with a fresh check. These need new saved data.

"Round 10" is not a question. It is the number of this set of questions. We have asked you 9 sets before this one, so this is set 10.

**Context**

1. You and I, and Codex, found 7 new features for Classroom Home.
2. Two of the 7 features can go in the same place: the large card in the middle of the page. That card is the main insight.
3. Only one of them can be at the top of that card. Question 1 asks: which one?

**The two features**

- **Diagnosis to action.** The app looks at the student's recent work. It tells the student what type of practice to do next. Example: "Your misses are fundamentals. Do the Necessary Assumption lesson in the bootcamp."
- **Coaching attached to the work.** You write a short note on the card. The student sees your words and your name. Example: "Joshua: Slow down on the stimulus. Do 5 of these untimed first." An "Ask" button also lets the student send you a question about the card.

**Question 1, in simple words**

When a student opens Classroom Home, what must they see first on the main card?

- **Option A: your note first.** Your note is at the top. The app's recommendation is below it. If you did not write a note, the app's recommendation is at the top.
- **Option B: the app's recommendation first.** Your note is below it, smaller.

**My recommendation: Option A.** Students pay for you. Your words must be the first thing they read. When you have no note, the page still works because the app's recommendation moves to the top.

**What to reply**

Reply "A" or "B" for question 1. You can reply "yes" to use my recommendations for questions 2 to 7, or tell me which ones to change.

Here are the six open questions again. Each one says what it means, gives an example, then asks.

---

**Q2 - The "Today" list**

**What it means:** "Today" is a short to-do list at the top of Classroom Home that the app writes for the student each day. Example:

> Today, about 35 minutes
> 1. Redo 4 wrong answers (10 min)
> 2. Finish your assigned work (15 min)
> 3. 8 Flaw questions (10 min)
> [Start]

The student presses Start and the app takes them through all three in order.

**The question:** How important is this list?
- **A.** It's the main way to use the page, and everything flows through it.
- **B.** It's an extra shortcut. Students can use it or ignore it, and every card still works on its own.

**Recommended: B** for the first version. If students use it a lot, we make it bigger later.

---

**Q3 - Your list for "what to do next"**

**What it means:** After a section, the app already sorts a student's mistakes into four kinds: reading, fundamentals, process, and test-taking. To recommend the right next step, it needs a list that says, for example:
- "fundamentals mistake on Necessary Assumption" goes to "Assumption bootcamp, Day 2"
- "reading mistake" goes to "Read, Recall, Colour"

**The question:** Will you check that list? I'd write the first draft from your bootcamps and guided drills, about 4 to 8 lines, and you'd fix anything wrong. That takes you one time about 15 minutes.

**Recommended: yes.** Without it, the app can only say "practice more of this type".

---

**Q4 - Asking how sure the student is**

**What it means:** The "Sure but wrong" card shows questions a student was confident about and still missed. For that, the drill has to ask "How sure are you?" before they see the answer. Some parts of the app don't ask this today.

**The question:** Should every drill question show a small, optional "How sure are you?" tap before the answer? Skipping it never blocks the student.

**Recommended: yes.**

---

**Q5 - The session brief**

**What it means:** Before a session with you, the student can open the Next session card and pick up to 3 questions they want to talk about, plus a short note. For example: "PT 101 S2 Q14, I don't get why C is wrong." They press Share and you see it before the session. If they never press Share, you see nothing.

**The question:** Do you want this?

**Recommended: yes.** You walk into sessions knowing what they're stuck on, and it's no extra work for you.

---

**Q6 - Two layout questions**

**(a) The left menu.** The v3 design shows a thin menu of icons only. Today's menu has full words. The menu is shared by every Classroom page, so changing it changes the whole Classroom, not only Home.
**The question:** Keep today's full menu for now?
**Recommended: yes, keep it.**

**(b) Students with little data.** Some cards will have nothing to show for a new student. The rules would be:
- only 1 or 2 question types practiced: show one simple result instead of the circles
- no practice at all: show "Start your first Smart Drill"
- no session booked or no assignment: hide that card instead of showing an empty box

**The question:** Use these rules?
**Recommended: yes.**

---

**Q7 - What gets built first**

**What it means:** We have a lot to build, so it goes in two builds.
- **First build:**
  - the v3 look
  - honest charts
  - fast loading and phone layout
  - the Ask button
  - "what to do next" recommendations
  - "Sure but wrong"

  These use data the app already has.
- **Second build:**
  - the Today list
  - the session brief
  - "redo, then try a new question"

  These need the app to start saving new information.

**The question:** Is that split right?
**Recommended: yes.**

---

**Q2 — B: optional shortcut.** Every card should work independently, permanently—not just in version one. Today can assemble a useful sequence, but students must remain free to continue homework, review a mistake, or start practice directly. Diagnosis-to-action remains the main card’s job.

**Q3 — Yes to a small registry, but map specific findings, not just category × type.** Claude should draft 4–8 mappings from existing diagnosis rules to verified resources, including the reason each resource fits. Joshua reviews those once. Broad categories can hide different problems, so an unsupported finding should open its evidence rather than manufacture a recommendation. New mappings need review only when added.

**Q4 — Use existing ratings first; don’t add another interaction to every question yet.** First verify when ratings were collected and whether enough usable data exists. If coverage is insufficient, pilot an optional confidence tap in untimed practice, after selecting an answer but before submitting it. Keep it out of timed sections initially. I disagree with adding it everywhere before establishing its value.

**Q5 — Yes, with one important boundary.** Students choose up to three questions and an optional note, then explicitly share the brief. Joshua sees it beside the booking in the Hub. Sharing should mean “for our session,” with no implied obligation to reply beforehand. No automatic collection and no routine coach writing.

**Q6a — Keep the full shared menu.** Changing navigation across the Classroom is a separate project, and a slim rail is not necessary to achieve v3’s visual quality. Design Home around the real available width, including the existing mobile menu behavior.

**Q6b — Agree on simpler results; disagree with hiding every empty session card.** With one or two eligible types, show labelled results instead of bubbles; insufficient evidence gets a practice invitation. Hide an absent assignment. When no session is booked, show a compact booking action if the student has access, rather than making scheduling disappear. Loading or failed data must never masquerade as emptiness.

**Q7 — Change the split slightly.** First ship v3’s layout, honest visuals, the quality bar, a small diagnosis registry, and Ask opening an evidence-attached draft in the existing inbox. Ship sure-but-wrong only if the ratings audit establishes usable coverage; otherwise defer it. Second, build the student-controlled session brief, then review plus a fresh check, then Today. I would prioritise those concrete learning and coaching functions over building the sequence that groups them. Routine coach notes are unnecessary in either release.

# Codex-Review — Adversarial Plan-Review Loop

Two models, one plan, a bounded argument. **Claude is the builder and orchestrator. Codex is a read-only critic** that can read the repo and the plan but cannot touch a single file. They communicate strictly through `PLAN.md` + a Codex session that persists across rounds. The human enters at exactly two points: kickoff and final sign-off.

This is a **deliberate, high-stakes tool** — reach for it on auth, data models, concurrency, migrations, payments, anything expensive to get wrong. Skip it for obvious/cheap work.

## Prerequisites (verify once, fast)

- Codex CLI installed and recent: `codex --version` (need ≥ 0.130; the default `gpt-5.5` model errors on older CLIs).
- Codex authenticated: a prior `codex login` (ChatGPT account is fine). If a run returns an auth/model error, surface it to the user — do not silently retry.
- Do NOT pin `-m` unless the user asks. The user's `~/.codex/config.toml` default model is used. Pinning `gpt-5.x-codex` variants fails on ChatGPT-account auth.
- **Echo the active model before Round 1** so the user can confirm: read the `model` line from `~/.codex/config.toml` (absent = "CLI default"); state it with the resolved tunables. If the user objects, stop before burning a round.
- **Sandbox flag differs between the two commands.** `codex exec` accepts `-s read-only`. `codex exec resume` does NOT — it rejects `-s` ("unexpected argument"). On resume you MUST force read-only via `-c sandbox_mode="read-only"`, because `config.toml` may default `sandbox_mode` to `danger-full-access` (+ `approval_policy="never"`) — which would let Codex WRITE files mid-loop. This is the single most important safety detail in this skill: verified end-to-end on 2026-06-04.

## Tunable variables (read from skill args, else default)

| Var | Default | Meaning |
|-----|---------|---------|
| `MAX_ROUNDS` | `5` | Hard cap on review rounds. The loop ALWAYS terminates at this. |
| `PLAN_FILE` | `PLAN.md` | Where the evolving plan lives (repo root). |
| `LOG_FILE` | `PLAN-REVIEW-LOG.md` | Append-only transcript of the argument (every round's critique + what changed). The artifact. |

If the user invoked the skill with an argument like `rounds=3`, use that for `MAX_ROUNDS`. Echo the resolved values back before starting.

## Flow

### Step 0 — Kickoff (human gate #1)

The invocation itself is the kickoff. Confirm scope in one line: what is being planned. If the user gave no task, ask for it (one question). Then proceed — do NOT ask for approval round-by-round; that comes at the end.

### Step 1 — Claude plans

Do real planning: read the relevant code, think through the approach, surface decisions and tradeoffs. Then write the plan to `PLAN_FILE` in this structure:

```markdown
# Plan: <task>
_Round 0 — initial draft by Claude_

## Goal
<one paragraph>

## Approach
<numbered steps, concrete>

## Key decisions & tradeoffs
<the contestable choices — name them explicitly so Codex has something to bite>

## Risks / open questions
<what you're unsure about>

## Out of scope
<bounds>
```

Initialize `LOG_FILE`:
```markdown
# Plan Review Log: <task>
Started <stamp the user's local time if known, else "session start">. MAX_ROUNDS=<n>.
```

Show the user the plan inline and say you're sending it to Codex for adversarial review.

### Step 2 — The loop

Maintain `ROUND` (start 1) and `THREAD_ID` (empty until round 1 returns).

**The review prompt** sent to Codex each round (adjust the task line):

> You are an adversarial reviewer for an implementation plan. Be skeptical and specific — your job is to find what breaks, not to be agreeable. Read the plan at `PLAN.md` (and any repo files you need; you are read-only). Identify concrete flaws: security holes, race conditions, missing edge cases, schema conflicts, wrong assumptions, observability gaps, simpler alternatives. For each, give a one-line fix. Do NOT modify any files. End your reply with EXACTLY one line: `VERDICT: APPROVED` if the plan is sound enough to implement, or `VERDICT: REVISE` if it still has material problems.

**Round 1** (creates the session — capture `thread_id`):

```bash
codex exec -s read-only --json \
  -o /tmp/codex-verdict.txt \
  "$(cat REVIEW_PROMPT)" \
  < /dev/null 2>/dev/null | grep '"type":"thread.started"'
```
Parse `thread_id` from the `{"type":"thread.started","thread_id":"..."}` line → that is `THREAD_ID`. The critique text lands in `/tmp/codex-verdict.txt` (Codex's last message). Read that file.

> Note: stderr carries cosmetic MCP/auth noise on some setups — `2>/dev/null` is intentional. Confirm success by the presence of the verdict file + a `thread.started` line. If neither appears, the run failed (auth/model) — stop and tell the user.
>
> **`< /dev/null` is mandatory:** `codex exec` reads stdin *in addition to* the prompt arg, so under a non-interactive driver (Claude Code's Bash tool, CI, any non-TTY pipeline) it blocks forever waiting on stdin EOF — a silent ~0% CPU hang. The redirect gives it immediate EOF. Required on the resume call below too.
>
> **Timeout guard:** run every `codex exec` / `codex exec resume` with a 10-minute ceiling so any future stall fails loud instead of hanging silently. Via Claude Code's Bash tool, pass `timeout: 600000` on the tool call (the default 2-minute tool timeout is too short for real reviews and would kill them mid-run). In a plain shell, prefix the command with `timeout 600` (Linux / Git Bash) or `gtimeout 600` (macOS via coreutils — stock macOS has no `timeout`). If the ceiling trips, treat it as a failed run: stop and tell the user rather than retrying blind. Applies to the resume call below too.

**Rounds 2..MAX** (resume the SAME session — Codex remembers its earlier critiques, won't re-litigate settled points):

```bash
# NOTE: resume rejects -s. Force read-only via -c sandbox_mode, or Codex
# inherits config.toml (possibly danger-full-access) and could write files.
codex exec resume "$THREAD_ID" -c sandbox_mode="read-only" --json \
  -o /tmp/codex-verdict.txt \
  "I revised the plan. Re-review PLAN.md. Same rules. End with VERDICT: APPROVED or VERDICT: REVISE." \
  < /dev/null 2>/dev/null >/dev/null
```

Both `codex exec` and `codex exec resume` support `--json` (stream → parse `thread_id` first round) and `-o/--output-last-message` (verdict capture).

**Each round, after Codex returns:**
1. Read `/tmp/codex-verdict.txt`. Append to `LOG_FILE`: `## Round <n> — Codex` + the full critique.
2. Grep the last line for the verdict token.
   - `VERDICT: APPROVED` → break the loop, go to Step 3 (converged).
   - `VERDICT: REVISE` → Claude reads the critique, decides **what's actually worth acting on** (Claude has final say — Codex advises, it does not command). Revise `PLAN_FILE`. Append to `LOG_FILE`: `### Claude's response` + what you changed and what you rejected and why. Increment `ROUND`.
3. If `ROUND > MAX_ROUNDS` → break to Step 3 (deadlock).
4. **Triage before you spend a round.** Sort every finding into one of three buckets first:
   - *Technical* — Claude decides and revises. Normal loop.
   - *Product / scope / pedagogy* — a decision only Joshua can make (what the feature should do, what tradeoff is acceptable, what is in scope). **Do not spend a round arguing it.** Stop the loop, put it to Joshua with `AskUserQuestion`, and fold his answer into `PLAN_FILE` before resuming. Rounds have been burned re-litigating decisions Codex was never able to settle.
   - *Already rejected* — see the re-assertion rule below.
5. **Re-assertion guard.** When a round returns a finding Claude rejected in an earlier round, do not re-argue it. Check whether the critique cites **new evidence** (a file, a line, a failure mode not previously raised). If not, log it as `re-assertion, no new evidence — held` and move on without revising. Carry the standing rejections into the resume prompt so Codex knows what has already been settled: append `Already settled (do not re-raise without new evidence): <list>` to each resume message.

### Step 3 — Resolution (human gate #2)

**If APPROVED:** Present to the user — the final `PLAN_FILE`, a 3-bullet summary of what the argument improved, and the round count. Ask: *"Plan survived N rounds of Codex. Implement it now — Codex builds it (`/codex-build`), Claude builds it, or stop here?"* Only on a yes is code written. **No code is written during the loop.** If the user picks Codex, invoke the `codex-build` skill with `SPEC_FILE=PLAN.md` and the same `LOG_FILE` — roles flip (Codex writes, Claude reviews the diff) and the build rounds append to the same log.

**If MAX_ROUNDS hit without APPROVED (deadlock):** Do NOT pretend it converged. Surface the unresolved disagreements explicitly: list each point Codex still flags and Claude's counter-position. Hand it to the human to break the tie. This is a legitimate, useful outcome — a flagged disagreement beats a false "approved."

## Hard rules

- Codex is read-only EVERY round — `-s read-only` for the first call, `-c sandbox_mode="read-only"` for every resume (resume has no `-s`). It never writes. If you're tempted to give it write access, stop — that's a different skill.
- The loop ALWAYS terminates at `MAX_ROUNDS`. No unbounded recursion.
- Claude is the final arbiter on every REVISE — incorporate good critiques, reject bad ones *with a reason logged*. Don't cave to Codex on everything (that defeats the cross-model check) and don't ignore it (that defeats the point).
- **Never spend a round on a decision only Joshua can make.** Product, scope, and pedagogy questions go to him immediately via `AskUserQuestion`, not into the argument. Codex cannot resolve them and each attempt costs a full repo re-read.
- **Rounds resume the SAME thread.** Every round after the first is `codex exec resume "$THREAD_ID"`. If `thread_id` capture failed, fix the capture — do not fall back to a fresh `codex exec`, which re-reads the whole repo and loses everything already settled. Observed rounds of 3 to 5 have mostly been this failure, not genuine disagreement.
- Code only after human gate #2.
- `LOG_FILE` is the deliverable — it tells the whole story of the argument. Keep it complete.

## What NOT to do

- Don't use this to review existing code — that's `/codex:review`.
- Don't pin a `-codex` model variant on ChatGPT-account auth — it 400s.
- Don't skip the log — the argument transcript is the most valuable artifact.
- Don't let Codex edit files. Read-only, always.

Companion `home_lr_type_evidence(p_class_id, p_qtype, p_days)` shares one SQL selection function and returns the qids.\n- **Sections**: Home adapter over `section_history` (not the zero-filling `sectionHistory.ts` reader): derives totals from `total_questions`/`initial_score`, unknown dates stay unknown, label \"Sections\" unless `mode` proves timing; blind review delta shown as evidence only.\n- **Section diagnosis**: top saved finding from `analysis_json` via `sectionAnalysis.ts`.\n- Selection: each lens returns null or `{score, evidence}`; floors (types: ≥8 distinct questions in window; sections: ≥1 in 21 days); score = recency + actionability, normalized per lens; fixed tie order; one card per source.\n- **Stability**: the chosen insight is frozen for the visit; new data shows a quiet \"New results, refresh\" control, never reorders under the pointer.\n\n### 3. Diagnosis-to-action (leads the main card)\n- A small registry `home/actionRegistry.ts`: 4-8 entries, each `{ findingRule, resource: launch body or route, reason }`, drafted by Claude from existing bootcamps, guided drills, Read Recall Colour and timed sections, reviewed once by Joshua before merge.\n- Main card order: headline (what the evidence shows), the recommended action with its one-line reason, then \"Practice <type>\" (`kind: 'filter'`, new questions preferred) and \"Review these\" (journal entries of that type, when any). An unmapped finding shows \"See the evidence\" instead of a recommendation.\n- Evidence drawer: exact question addresses from the evidence RPC or section `questions_json`; counts, window, \"updated <time>\".\n\n### 4. Honest visuals\n- Bubbles: area (not diameter) proportional to questions missed; every bubble labelled with type, accuracy and \"missed of attempted\"; a two-size legend; keyboard-selectable, selecting highlights the row in the list below; no bubbles when fewer than 3 eligible types (show labelled results instead).\n- Copy: \"Most misses in your recent LR practice\", never \"costing you points\".\n- List: continuous bars, question count per type, change \"pts vs previous 30 days\" suppressed when either window has <8 questions.\n- Every number carries what it counts and when it updated.\n\n### 5. Ask with evidence\n\"Ask\" on the main insight opens a draft in the existing inbox addressed to Joshua, pre-filled with the insight's evidence (types, counts, question addresses) and a free-text comment. Nothing sends without the student's explicit Send. Label is \"Ask\" (glossary rule).\n\n### 6. Sure but wrong (gated)\nFirst an audit query (read-only, run by Claude before build): share of `attempts` in the last 30 days with non-null `confidence`, and whether ratings are captured before feedback on each write path. Ship the card only if ≥60% coverage for at least half of active students; otherwise defer to build two. Card (when shipped): 2x2 labelled cells (sure/unsure x right/wrong, 1-2 unsure, 4-5 sure), coverage line, floor of 12 rated first attempts; tap a cell for its questions.\n\n### 7. Quality bar (release requirement)\n- Paint from TanStack Query cache on return visits; skeletons that match the real layout on first load; no spinners.\n- Loading, failed, stale and empty are distinct states; a failed RPC never shows a \"start here\" empty state.\n- Count-up numbers once per local day; all motion transform/opacity only; none under `prefers-reduced-motion`.\n- WCAG 2.2 AA: contrast on gradients and taupe, 200% text, focus order, chart text alternatives (the list is the accessible equivalent of the bubbles), no colour-only meaning.\n- Accommodations: any pace figure uses the student's own timer setting.\n- Verification: student test account at 390 / 900 / 1440 and docked widths, dark class, keyboard only, reduced motion.\n\n### 8. Build order (PRs)\n1. Layout + top panel + tiles on existing data, on top of #133 (closes #133).\n2. RPCs + adapters + insight engine + honest visuals + list + evidence drawer.\n3. Registry + diagnosis-to-action + Ask draft.\n4. Sure but wrong, only if the audit passes.\nBuild two (separate plan): session brief, review then fresh check, Today shortcut, coach pinning with optional note, unified notifications.\n\n## Key decisions & tradeoffs\n- Diagnosis leads; coach notes are never required (Joshua).\n- Bubbles kept for character, list below for precision.\n- Frozen insight per visit over live reordering.\n- Registry is small and human-reviewed rather than inferred.\n- Sure but wrong gated by a data audit rather than adding a new rating step.\n\n## Risks / open questions\n- RPC performance on Home's first paint; measure before/after.\n- Registry quality depends on Joshua's one review.\n- Student test account needed for visual verification.\n- Taupe/gradient contrast may force darker text than the mockup.\n\n## Out of scope\nSlim menu rail; notifications merge and bell popover; pinning; session brief; review loop; Today; goal score, streaks, PT score, month calendar; AI text; teacher view redesign; public landing page.\n"}

The plan needs revision before implementation. It still specifies the superseded release sequence, and several database and interaction contracts are incomplete.

1. **P1 — The build order contradicts the settled decisions.** [PLAN.md:96](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-23-classroom-home-PLAN.md:96) ships layout separately from insights. The [capture:135](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-23-classroom-home-visual-upgrade.md:135) explicitly supersedes PR #133 and combines them; [capture:154](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-23-classroom-home-visual-upgrade.md:154) also requires the finding-to-resource registry and evidence-attached Ask draft, neither of which appears in the plan.
   **Fix:** Replace the release sequence with the settled v3 split and explicitly enumerate the registry, Ask draft, and subsequent session-brief/review/Today work.

2. **P1 — The proposed message uniqueness constraint cannot be created as specified.** [PLAN.md:80](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-23-classroom-home-PLAN.md:80) requires `(recipient, event_key)` on both tables, but messages contain `conversation_id` and `sender_id`, with recipients represented through participants ([schema:24](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/projects/lsatu/supabase/migrations/20260416234013_49bfd508-03f2-4942-a636-2763f1f3af5b.sql:24)). Also, a unique index makes duplicate inserts fail; it does not make retries successful no-ops.
   **Fix:** Define actual per-table identity columns and explicit conflict handling, including recovery when only one of the two writes succeeds.

3. **P1 — Update immutability does not protect event identity at insertion.** [PLAN.md:81](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-23-classroom-home-PLAN.md:81) protects changes to `origin` and `event_key`, but the existing message INSERT policy permits participant-authored messages without restricting those new fields ([schema:110](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/projects/lsatu/supabase/migrations/20260416234013_49bfd508-03f2-4942-a636-2763f1f3af5b.sql:110)). A student could supply a system origin and colliding event key.
   **Fix:** Enforce ordinary-user inserts as `origin='human' AND event_key IS NULL`, reserve generated identities for authorized writers, and test forged inserts.

4. **P1 — Legacy generated events have no defined read transition.** [PLAN.md:82](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-23-classroom-home-PLAN.md:82) includes legacy rows with null keys, while the following paragraph acknowledges generated events exclusively by key. Those events cannot follow the specified acknowledgement path; legacy messages also lack a defined human/system classification.
   **Fix:** Give legacy feed events stable synthetic identities and explicit classification/read rules without rewriting historical rows.

5. **P1 — The two unread systems will disagree.** [PLAN.md:79](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-23-classroom-home-PLAN.md:79) leaves `useInbox` untouched, but its unread calculation ignores acknowledgements and uses the latest message ([useInbox.ts:192](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/projects/lsatu/src/hooks/useInbox.ts:192)). Opening a generated event can clear the Home bell while leaving the existing inbox unread; opening its conversation updates only the watermark, leaving the new event acknowledgement absent.
   **Fix:** Specify consistent read semantics across Home, the bell, and conversations, and adapt existing unread consumers through a shared selector.

6. **P1 — Rescheduled reminders reuse their old identity.** [PLAN.md:92](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-23-classroom-home-PLAN.md:92) promises re-keying on reschedule but defines the key only from booking ID and offset. Rescheduling the same booking after its first reminder makes the replacement collide with the old event or inherit its acknowledgement.
   **Fix:** Include a booking revision or scheduled-start timestamp in the key and revalidate that revision immediately before delivery.

7. **P1 — Fresh-practice actions ship before their dependency, and the named queue-building location is wrong.** [PLAN.md:72](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-23-classroom-home-PLAN.md:72) requires “Try new ones” for phase-two insights, while the filter extension sits with the later By-type work. [drillRunSpec.ts:146](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/projects/lsatu/src/lib/drillRunSpec.ts:146) merely constructs configuration; actual bank filtering occurs in [Drill.tsx:565](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/projects/lsatu/src/pages/Drill.tsx:565).
   **Fix:** Ship exposure exclusion and persisted-queue resolution before the insight CTA, applying exclusion before sampling and preserving that queue on resume.

8. **P1 — The new RPCs lack an authorization contract.** [PLAN.md:56](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-23-classroom-home-PLAN.md:56) accepts caller-supplied class IDs and returns question-level evidence, but specifies neither execution security nor cross-student tests; the feed RPC is similarly unspecified. Existing aggregate RPCs deliberately use `SECURITY INVOKER` ([migration:89](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/projects/lsatu/supabase/migrations/20260709235900_phase6_query_rpcs.sql:89)).
   **Fix:** Require invoker/RLS enforcement or explicit owner/admin authorization, pinned search paths, bounded inputs, and anonymous/cross-student denial tests for every new RPC.

9. **P2 — The design’s comparative statistics cannot come from the proposed response.** [Home3.dc.html:108](C:/Users/Joshu/AppData/Local/Temp/claude/C--Users-Joshu-dev-ai-os/fe553725-d881-4068-8809-1fb7b342eee9/scratchpad/artifact-files/307bbd6c-86e0-43cd-a5df-8735d306b8fb/project/Home3.dc.html:108) shows change against the previous 30 days; [PLAN.md:56](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-23-classroom-home-PLAN.md:56) returns one window. Requesting 60 days does not produce two comparable windows because latest-attempt deduplication can select different attempts.
   **Fix:** Return separately defined current/prior buckets with a shared cutoff and sample floors, and hide comparisons lacking sufficient evidence.

10. **P2 — The visual contract omits truthful bubble sizing and accessible labels.** [Home3.dc.html:73](C:/Users/Joshu/AppData/Local/Temp/claude/C--Users-Joshu-dev-ai-os/fe553725-d881-4068-8809-1fb7b342eee9/scratchpad/artifact-files/307bbd6c-86e0-43cd-a5df-8735d306b8fb/project/Home3.dc.html:73) claims circles encode missed questions, but their diameters are hardcoded. The primary bubble also uses white labels over orange; white against `#ff8906` is approximately **2.38:1**. Keyboard testing alone will not catch that.
    **Fix:** Specify area-proportional sizing, readable text independent of bubble size, a textual equivalent, and contrast checks across gradients and themes.

11. **P2 — Empty, failed, and unavailable data remain conflated.** [PLAN.md:66](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-23-classroom-home-PLAN.md:66) offers only eligible evidence or `null`, then converts zero eligible lenses into “Start here.” A failed reader can therefore masquerade as no practice, and a failed pinned source can become “pinned view has no data,” contradicting [capture:153](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-23-classroom-home-visual-upgrade.md:153).
    **Fix:** Define loading/error/empty/insufficient/ready adapter states and prohibit failure or pending data from producing empty-state claims.

12. **P2 — RC history reuse risks truncated results and unnecessary downloads.** [PLAN.md:59](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-23-classroom-home-PLAN.md:59) points to `rcProgress.ts`, whose `getQuestionHistory()` requests all attempts without pagination and whose `listSessions()` requests all sessions. Parallelizing these reads does not make them bounded or guarantee complete results.
    **Fix:** Add a bounded Home aggregate that explicitly handles first attempts, excluded sessions, completion, and recency; fetch detailed evidence only when opened.

13. **P2 — The RC launcher duplicates an existing section launch path.** [PLAN.md:46](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-23-classroom-home-PLAN.md:46) proposes new session creation, but [rcSections.ts:131](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/projects/lsatu/src/features/rc/lib/rcSections.ts:131) already launches complete sections with accommodations, untimed mode, and end-of-set reveal. PR #133 also does not supply the promised Section tab: at branch commit `8b4c406`, `ClassroomHomeView.tsx:287` only opens the existing finder.
    **Fix:** Reuse the existing RC section contract, extend custom timing where necessary, and explicitly build/test Section-tab selection rather than treating PR #133 as that dependency.

Read-only review; no files modified. Findings are based on the supplied documents and source inspection, not runtime or deployed-database verification.

VERDICT: REVISE

/**
 * The link that starts a section. Uses the existing hub contract, so an

 * `end_of_set` because a section is sat under test conditions, with review
 * afterwards rather than between passages.
 */
export function rcSectionHref(
  section: RcSection,
  paceId: RcSectionPaceId = 'standard',
  reveal: RcRevealMode = 'end_of_set',
): string {
  const pace = rcSectionPace(paceId);

Reviewed the saved **“Act 5, Round 1 revision”**. Unified notifications remain excluded. These issues still need correction:

1. **P1 — The section adapter trusts a known bad legacy denominator.** [PLAN.md:35](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-23-classroom-home-PLAN.md:35) derives totals from `total_questions`, but [sectionHistorySave.ts:324](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/projects/lsatu/src/lib/sectionHistorySave.ts:324) documents historical rows carrying zero there while `initial_total` contains the real total. Valid work can become `19/0` or disappear as insufficient.
   **Fix:** Prefer valid `initial_total`, fall back to positive `total_questions`, preserve unknown denominators, and require `br_used` before showing blind-review results.

2. **P1 — Shared SQL logic does not guarantee matching evidence snapshots.** [PLAN.md:32](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-23-classroom-home-PLAN.md:32) uses `now()` for each RPC, while [line 42](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-23-classroom-home-PLAN.md:42) freezes the insight for the visit. Opening evidence later can include a new attempt, replace the selected attempt for a question, or exclude one crossing the window boundary.
   **Fix:** Return a snapshot cutoff with the aggregate and pass it to evidence queries, retaining selected attempt IDs where necessary.

3. **P1 — Fresh-only launches can resume an unrestricted queue.** [PLAN.md:47](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-23-classroom-home-PLAN.md:47) adds `excludeAnswered`, but the existing resume fingerprint includes only the current filter fields ([drillResumeSession.ts:93](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/projects/lsatu/src/lib/drillResumeSession.ts:93)). Identical filters with different freshness settings therefore share a resume record unless explicitly changed.
   **Fix:** Include freshness policy in the resume identity, preserve existing identities for unrestricted links, and test launching fresh-only after an unfinished unrestricted run.

4. **P1 — “Timed section” still cannot start a timed section.** [PLAN.md:22](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-23-classroom-home-PLAN.md:22) deliberately retains #133’s finder action while deferring section launching to build two. On `feat/home-v2-phase1`, `ClassroomHomeView.tsx:287` only calls `open()`; the finder returns individual questions/passages. This leaves a promised primary action without its advertised destination.
   **Fix:** Route the button to an existing working section-selection surface now; defer the new launcher tab independently.

5. **P1 — Keyboard-selectable bubbles cannot also be hidden from assistive technology.** [PLAN.md:51](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-23-classroom-home-PLAN.md:51) makes bubbles interactive, while [line 53](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-23-classroom-home-PLAN.md:53) applies `aria-hidden`. A summary label does not restore hidden controls, leaving keyboard focus on elements screen readers cannot identify.
   **Fix:** Either expose labelled bubble controls with selection state, or make bubbles entirely noninteractive and provide selection through accessible list controls.

6. **P2 — Perfect accuracy produces invisible bubbles.** [PLAN.md:51](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-23-classroom-home-PLAN.md:51) specifies `radius = k * sqrt(missed)` but only falls back when fewer than three types qualify. Three sufficiently practised types with zero misses qualify yet produce zero-radius circles; mixed results also lose labels for perfect types.
   **Fix:** Define an all-zero-misses result state and represent zero-miss types in the list without inventing a minimum bubble area.

7. **P2 — Confidence level 3 falls outside the promised matrix.** [PLAN.md:61](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-23-classroom-home-PLAN.md:61) counts non-null confidence for coverage and requires 12 rated attempts, but its matrix includes only ratings 1–2 and 4–5. Twelve neutral ratings can satisfy the floor while populating no cells, and displayed totals can disagree with the coverage line.
   **Fix:** Explicitly exclude and disclose neutral ratings, calculating the matrix’s eligibility floor from attempts actually represented in its cells.

No files modified; findings are based on source inspection, not deployed-data verification.

VERDICT: REVISE

The plan review is finished, and Codex and I don't disagree on anything, so there's nothing for you to referee.

**How it went:** 3 rounds with gpt-6-astra, read-only. It never said APPROVED, but I accepted every problem it found, and I checked the important ones in the code myself. Its first round reviewed the old plan file by mistake, because my rewrite hadn't saved. I caught that and re-ran it on the right file. The last fix went in after round 3, so Codex hasn't seen it.

**Plan:** [2026-09-23-classroom-home-PLAN.md](../brainstorms/2026-09-23-classroom-home-PLAN.md). Full record: [PLAN-REVIEW-LOG.md](../brainstorms/2026-09-23-classroom-home-PLAN-REVIEW-LOG.md).

**What the review changed:**
- **"Questions this week" would have been wrong.** The number came from a table nothing writes to (I confirmed this in the code), so it would have shown zero or a stale count. It now counts the questions students actually answered.
- **"Timed section" goes to the Testing Centre.** It already starts both LR and RC timed sections with accommodations. Before, the button only opened the search bar.
- **"Practice new questions only" really gives new questions.** It won't resume an older unfiltered run by mistake, and if there aren't enough new questions it says so before starting.
- **The numbers stay honest:**
  - "Last section" never shows "19/0" from old records, and shows blind review only when the student actually did one.
  - "See the evidence" always matches the number shown.
  - "Sure but wrong" doesn't count neutral ratings.
  - A failed or still-loading card never tells a student "start here".
- **Circles:** sized honestly. Types with no misses appear only in the list. No white text on orange, which was too hard to read. Screen readers get the list instead of the circles.
- **Security:** the new data functions follow the same access rules as the existing ones, and tests prove one student can't see another's data.
- **Deferred to the second build:** the notification merge and RC insights. The notification merge had several unsolved problems, so it gets designed once, properly.

**The first build, in 4 PRs:**
1. Layout (replaces PR #133).
2. The insight data and visuals.
3. "What to do next" plus the Ask button.
4. "Sure but wrong", only if the confidence-rating check passes.

**Your one job in it:** before PR 3, review a short list I'll draft, about 4 to 8 lines, matching each kind of mistake to the lesson or drill you'd assign. About 15 minutes.

**Should gpt-6-sol at low effort start PR 1 now?** I'll review each PR, check it visually, and bring each one to you before merging. It would help to have a student test account I can sign in with, so I can see the student view, not just yours.

function daysUntil(iso: string) {
  const start = new Date(iso);
  start.setHours(0, 0, 0, 0);
  const today = new Date();
  today.setHours(0, 0, 0, 0);
  return Math.round((start.getTime() - today.getTime()) / 86_400_000);
}

function untilLabel(iso: string) {
  const days = daysUntil(iso);
  if (days <= 0) return "today";
  if (days === 1) return "tomorrow";
  return `in ${spell(days)} days`;
}
